Global stability of the Minkowski spacetime for the Einstein-Vlasov system Thanks: Acknowledgement: On various stages of this work, the author was benefited a lot from many discussions with Alexandru Ionescu and Benoit Pausader. The author is supported partially by NSFC-12141102, and MOST-2020YFA0713003.
Xuecheng Wang

Abstract
. We prove global stability of the Minkowski spacetime in the wave coordinates system for the massive Einstein-Vlasov system. In particular, compared with previous results by Lindblad-Taylor [ 25 ] , in which the Vlasov part is assumed to have compact support assumption, and Fajman-Joudioux-Smulevci [ 15 ] , in which the spacetime is assumed to be exact Schwarzschild in the exterior region, we do not impose any compact support condition for the Vlasov part and allow a large class of non-isotropic perturbations for the metric part, which decay at rate ⟨ r ⟩ − 1 + \langle r\rangle^{-1+} towards space infinity.



1.
Introduction

We are interested in global stability problem for the Einstein field equations. One of the astrophysically important matter fields is dust like collisionless matter, which is described by the Vlasov equation. There is a vast literature on this topic, we refer readers to Andréasson
[
1
]
, Rendall
[
40
]
, and Stewart
[
45
]
for more on the backgrounds.

Mathematically speaking, we consider the stability of the Minkowski spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{1+3},\eta)
in the presence of the massive collisionless particles, where
\eta=\textup{diag}(-1,1,1,1)
. The unknown Lorentzian manifold
(M,g)
has signature
(-,+,+,+)
. We assume that the particle system satisfies classical equations of motion (called on shell), which means that the energy-momentum relation as defined in (
1.1
) holds for the four-momentum
(v^{0},v^{1},v^{2},v^{3}).
The
mass shell

\mathcal{M}
, which is a subbundle of the tangent space
TM
, is defined as follows,

\mathcal{M}:=\{(t,x^{i},v^{0},v^{i})\in TM:(v^{0},v^{i})\textup{\, is future directed},v^{\nu}v_{\nu}=-1\},

(1.1)

where, as in the standard notation, Greek indices run over
0,1,2,3
and Latin indices run over
1,2,3
. In the definition of mass shell, we have already normalized the speed of light
c
and the mass of particles
m
to be
1
.

The Einstein-Vlasov system, which describes the dynamics of spacetime and the distribution function of particles, reads as follows,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\textup{Ric}(g)-\frac{1}{2}R(g)g=T(f)\\
\\
T_{g}f=0,\end{array}\right.

(1.2)

where
\textup{Ric}(g)
is the Ricci curvature tensor of
g
,
R(g)
is the scalar curvature of
g
,
T(f)
is the energy-momentum tensor associated with the distribution of particles
f:\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
T_{g}
is the generator of the geodesic flow of the Lorentzian manifold
(M,g)
.

The main goal of this paper is to prove the global stability of
(\eta,0)
in wave coordinates for the massive Einstein-Vlasov system for unrestricted initial data. Compared with previous results by Lindblad-Taylor
[
25
]
, in which the metric is assumed to be asymptotically flat and the Vlasov part has compact support assumption, and Fajman-Joudioux-Smulevci
[
15
]
, in which the metric is assumed to be exact Schwarzchild spacetime in the exterior region and there is no compact support assumption on the Vlasov part, we do not require any compact support assumption for the Vlasov part and allow a large class of non-isotropic perturbations. Indeed, our assumptions on the metric on the initial slice are essentially of the following type

g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}+\epsilon\mathcal{O}(\langle r\rangle^{-1+}),\quad\partial g_{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon\mathcal{O}(\langle r\rangle^{-2+}),

(1.3)

which is slightly better than the asymptotically flat assumption of the metric.

We use the framework developed by Ionescu-Pausader
[
29
]
in the study of the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system, which allows us to combine the strength of both the vector fields method and the Fourier method. The Fourier analysis is mainly used in the analysis of resonant sets and a special “designer” norm (
Z
-norm), to prove the decay estimate over time for both the metric part and the density-type function for particles in lower space regularity norms.

1.1.
The PDE formulation in wave coordinates

Due to the differeomorphism covariance of the Einstein-Vlasov system, we choose the so-called wave coordinates. By working in this wave coordinate system, the Einstein equations are reduced into a system of quasilinear wave equations. We refer readers to
[
29
]
and Wald
[
47
]
for related discussions.

Recall that the wave coordinate system
\{x^{\mu}\}_{\mu},
satisfies the following equation

\square_{g}x^{\mu}=0.

(1.4)

From (
1.4
), we have

\Gamma_{\mu}=g^{\alpha\beta}g_{\nu\mu}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\nu}=g^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\beta}\big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}+\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\mu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\big)=0.

(1.5)

Recall that the connection coefficients are defined as follow,

\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\beta}:=g(\partial_{\mu},\nabla_{\partial_{\beta}}\partial_{\alpha})=\frac{1}{2}\big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\beta\mu}+\partial_{\beta}g_{\alpha\mu}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\big),

where
\nabla
is the torsion free covariant derivative. In the wave coordinates system, we have

2R_{\alpha\beta}=-g^{\gamma\mu}\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}+Q_{\alpha\beta}+P_{\alpha\beta},

(1.6)

where

Q_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\mu}g^{\lambda\nu}\big[\big(\partial_{\lambda}g_{\alpha\mu}\partial_{\nu}g_{\gamma\beta}+\partial_{\mu}g_{\lambda\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}g_{\nu\beta}\big)+2\big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}g_{\gamma\beta}-\partial_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\gamma\beta}

+\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}g_{\gamma\alpha}-\partial_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\beta}g_{\gamma\alpha}\big)+\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\gamma\beta}+\partial_{\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}\partial_{\beta}g_{\gamma\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\lambda\nu}\partial_{\mu}g_{\gamma\beta}-\partial_{\beta}g_{\lambda\nu}\partial_{\mu}g_{\gamma\alpha}\big)

+\big(\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\lambda}\partial_{\mu}g_{\gamma\beta}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\lambda\alpha}\partial_{\nu}g_{\gamma\beta}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\beta\lambda}\partial_{\mu}g_{\gamma\alpha}-\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\gamma}\partial_{\mu}g_{\lambda\beta}\big)\big],

(1.7)

P_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\mu}g^{\lambda\nu}\big[\big(-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\lambda}\partial_{\beta}g_{\gamma\nu}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\gamma}\partial_{\beta}g_{\lambda\nu}\big].

(1.8)

We consider the Vlasov part in (
1.2
). Let
\pi:\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow M
denote the canonical projection. The energy-momentum tensor in local coordinate system is given as follows,

T_{\mu\nu}(f)(x^{\alpha})=\int_{\pi^{-1}(x^{\alpha})}f(x^{\alpha},v^{\alpha})v_{\mu}v_{\nu}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det g|}v^{0}}dv_{i},

(1.9)

where the volume form
1/\big({\sqrt{|\det g|}v^{0}}\big)dv_{i}
is the induced volume form of the hypersurface
\pi^{-1}(x^{\alpha})\subset T_{x^{\alpha}}M
, the tangent space
T_{x^{\alpha}}M
is endowed with the metric
g_{\mu\nu}(x^{\alpha})dv^{\mu}dv^{\nu}
.

We derive a precise formula for the geodesic spray
T_{g}
. Let
(U,x^{\alpha})
be a local coordinate system on
M
, we define
the conjugate coordinate system
(x^{\alpha},v^{\alpha})
on
TM
, where
(x^{\alpha},v^{\alpha})
denotes the point
v^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}|_{(x^{\alpha})}\in TM.
Note that massive particles travel along timelike geodesics. Suppose that the geodesic is parametrized by
x^{\alpha}:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow M
for some
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

f(x^{\alpha}(s),v^{1}(s),v^{2}(s),v^{3}(s))=f(x^{\alpha}(0),v^{1}(0),v^{2}(0),v^{3}(0)).

Therefore, we have

\frac{dx^{\alpha}}{dt}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}f+\frac{dv^{i}(t)}{dt}\partial_{v^{i}}f=0,\qquad\Longrightarrow\big(v^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}-\Gamma^{i}_{\alpha\beta}v^{\alpha}v^{\beta}\partial_{v^{i}}\big)f=0,

(1.10)

and
the geodesic spray
T_{g}
can be written as follows,

T_{g}=v^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}-v^{\alpha}v^{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{i}\partial_{v^{i}},\quad\Gamma^{i}_{\alpha\beta}=g^{\mu i}\Gamma_{\mu\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu i}\big(\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\beta}+\partial_{\beta}g_{\mu\alpha}-\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}\big).

(1.11)

To find a favorable decomposition inside the acceleration term in (
1.10
), which is stated in Lemma
2.1
, we choose to work in the cotangent bundle and change coordinates system as follows,

(x^{\alpha},v^{i})\longrightarrow(x^{\alpha},v_{i}),\qquad\tilde{f}(x^{\alpha},v_{i}):=f(x^{\alpha},v^{i}(x^{\alpha},v_{j})),

where
v^{i}(x^{\alpha},v_{j}):=g^{i\alpha}v_{\alpha}.
In the new coordinates system
(x^{\alpha},v_{i})
, the Vlasov equation (
1.10
) reads as follows,

v^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\alpha}}\tilde{f}-\frac{1}{2}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\partial_{x^{j}}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{j}}\tilde{f}=0.

(1.12)

Without causing any confusion, we still use
f
instead of
\tilde{f}
to denote the distribution function of particles in terms of the coordinate system
(x^{\alpha},v_{i})
.

Moreover, from (
1.2
) and (
1.9
), we derive the following equation for the scalar curvature,

-R=g^{\mu\nu}T_{\mu\nu}(f)=-\int_{\pi^{-1}(x^{\alpha})}f(x^{\alpha},v_{i})\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det g|}v^{0}}dv_{i}:=T(f).

(1.13)

1.1.1.
The initial data set and the constraint equations

Assume that initially we have a spacelike hypersurface
(\Sigma,\bar{g},k)
, where
\bar{g}
is the Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
,
k
is the second fundamental form of the surface
\Sigma
, and initial particle distribution function
f_{0}:\pi^{-1}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+},
where
\pi:\mathcal{M}\longrightarrow M
is the natural projection. The following constraint equations must hold on
\Sigma
,

\overline{div}k_{j}-(d\overline{tr}k)_{j}=T_{0j}(f_{0}),\qquad\overline{R}+(\overline{tr}k)^{2}-|k|_{\bar{g}}^{2}=2T_{00}(f_{0}),

(1.14)

where
\overline{div}
,
\overline{tr}
,
\overline{R}
denote the divergence operator, trace operator, and the scalar curvature of
\bar{g}
respectively.

1.2.
Previous results

Global stability of physical solutions is an important topic in General Relativity. The ground breaking work of Christodoulou-Klainerman
[
11
]
shows the global nonlinear stability of the Minkowski spacetime for the Einstein-vacuum equation, see also Klainerman-Nicolò
[
34
]
, Lindblad-Rodnianski
[
23
,
24
]
, Bieri
[
4
]
, Speck
[
46
]
for various extensions on this line of research for the Einstein-vacuum equation. Moreover, from the work of Lindblad-Rodnianski
[
23
,
24
]
, in which they gave an alternative and conceptually simpler proof of the stability of Minkowski spacetime in the wave coordinates system, we know that Einstein equations in harmonic coordinates satisfy weak null condition. This observation plays a key role in later study of Einstein-scalar field systems.

More recently, global stability of the Minkowski spacetime have also been proved for other coupled Einstein field equations, e.g., Zisper
[
52
]
considered the Einstein-Maxwell system, Speck
[
46
]
considered the
Einstein-nonlinear electromagnetic system, Lefloch-Ma
[
38
,
39
]
and Wang
[
48
]
considered the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system for the restricted data, which coincides with the Schwarzschild in the exterior region, Ionescu-Pausader
[
29
]
considered the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system for the
unrestricted
data.

For the Einstein-Vlasov system, Bigorgne-Fajman-Joudioux-Smulevici-Thaller
[
2
]
considered the massless case for general initial data, in which the particles travel at the same speed of light. For the massive case, Lindblad-Taylor
[
25
]
considered the initial data with the compact support in Vlasov part and asymptotically flat metric, Fajman-Joudioux-Smulevci
[
15
]
considered the initial data with the assumption of the exact Schwarzschild in the exterior region and without compact support assumption on the Vlasov part.

Due to the finite speed of propagation, the use of restricted initial data coupled with the hyperbolic foliation method, leads to significant simplifications of the global analysis, particularly at the level of proving decay estimates.

Our goal in this paper are to extend the scope of the framework developed by Ionescu-Pausader in
[
29
]
and to work with
unrestricted
initial data for the massive case.

1.3.
The Main result

To state main results of this paper, we first introduce several function spaces. Let
N_{0}=200,\gamma=10^{-22}
, we define the energy spaces
E_{l},l\in[0,N_{0}]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, by the following norms,

\|f\|_{E_{l}}:=\big(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-k_{-}+2\gamma k_{-}+2lk_{+}}\|P_{k}(f)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

(1.15)

We state the main theorem as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\delta:=10^{-20}
,
N_{0}=200,N_{1}=10^{3}N_{0}
,
\Sigma=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:t=0\}
,
(\bar{g},k,f_{0})
be an initial data of the Einstein-Vlasov system (
1.2
) on
\Sigma
such that the constrain equations in (
1.14
) are satisfied. There exists a sufficiently small constant
\epsilon_{0}
such that if the following smallness condition holds,

\sum_{n,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},n+l\leq N_{0}}\sum_{\Gamma\in\{S,L_{i},\Omega_{ij}\},|\alpha|\leq n}\|(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)\Gamma^{\alpha}(g_{\alpha\beta}-\eta_{\alpha\beta})\big|_{t=0}\|_{E_{l}}

+\sum_{|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|\leq N_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{x}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}}(1+|x|+|v|^{1/\delta})^{2N_{1}}|\nabla_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\nabla_{v}^{\alpha_{2}}f_{0}(x,v)|^{2}dxdv\leq\epsilon_{0},

then there exist globally defined wave coordinates
\{x^{\mu}\}
and a unique future geodesically complete solution
(g_{\alpha\beta},f)
of the Einstein-Vlasov system (
1.2
) in
M:=\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{4},t\geq 0\}
, with the given initial data
(\bar{g},k,f_{0})
on
\Sigma
.

1.4.
Main ideas of the proof

The mechanism of the small data global regularity for the Einstein-Vlasov system consists of two parts:

(i)

Control of the
L^{2}
-type high order energy, which involves the high order Sobolev norm and the vector fields. For the Vlasov part, it also involves well-chosen weight functions to be propagated adapted to the hierarchy of space derivatives and vector fields.

(ii)

Prove almost sharp
L^{\infty}_{x}
-type decay estimates for the perturbed metric and the density type function of the Vlasov part.

We introduce some main ideas used in the above two main steps.

1.4.1.
Energy estimate for the wave part

As in
[
29
]
, to exploit the benefit of the harmonic gauge condition, which allows us to identify clearly the null structure inside the Einstein’s equation, we also use the double Hodge-decomposition variables
\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\Omega_{j},\rho,\vartheta_{mn}\}
.

Comparing with the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system studied in
[
29
]
, an extra benefit is that the scaling vector field
S
commutes with the Einstein-Vlasov system. We exploit this benefit and set the hierarchy for the energy functionals based on the total number of space derivatives and the total number of the vector fields applied on the profiles of the metric part.

To keep the discussion concise and highlight the main difficulties in the Einstein-Vlasov system, we refer readers to
[
29
]
for more details on the discussion of the wave-wave type interaction that comes from purely the Einstein-vacuum equations.

We focus on the discussion of the main ideas used in the wave-Vlasov type interaction in the Einstein’s equation, which mainly comes from the energy-momentum tensor determined by the Vlasov part.

Firstly, due to the linear density type function in the energy-momentum tensor, we modify the profile of the metric, which roughly defined as follows
U:=(\partial_{t}-i|\nabla|)h_{\alpha\beta}
. Roughly speaking, we have

(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)U=\rho(f)+\textup{nonlinear},\quad\rho(f):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)dv,

where
\rho(f)
is a density type function that is linear in
f
.

To capture the nonlinear effect of
\rho(f)
over time, we modified the profile
U
as follows

\widetilde{U}:=U+T(f),\quad T(f):=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\frac{\widehat{f}(t,\xi,v)}{i(|\xi|-\hat{v}\cdot\xi)}d\xi dv,\quad\hat{v}:=\frac{v}{\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}}.

As a result, we remove the linear density and have

(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)\widetilde{U}=\textup{nonlinear}.

In certain sense, we view
\widetilde{U}
as the metric part less effected by the Vlasov part and view
U
to be the sum of two parts: (i) the modified profile part
\widetilde{U}
; (ii) the linear density type part, which depends purely on the distribution function of the Vlasov part.

Motivated by this idea, we allow the Vlasov part to grow faster than the the modified profile in our bootstrap assumption. Roughly speaking, the modified profile grows at rate
\langle t\rangle^{\delta_{1}}
and the Vlasov part grows at rate
\langle t\rangle^{\delta_{0}}
, where
\delta_{1}\ll\delta_{0}.

There are two main ideas used in the energy estimate of the wave-Vlasov type interaction, which are also the main reasons why we can let the Vlasov part grows faster.

Firstly, thanks to the polynomial decay rate of the distribution function, we know that either the distribution function is small, i.e.,
|x|+|v|
large, or the distance with respect to the light cone is large, in which case the metric part decays faster. More precisely, we have

\langle|t|-|x+t\hat{v}|\rangle\langle|x|\rangle\langle|v|\rangle^{2}\gtrsim\langle t\rangle.

Therefore, if the frequency of the wave part is not too small, the decay rates are improved such that they are not near the critical “
1/\langle t\rangle
” rate.

Secondly, for the very low frequency of the wave part case, in which there is no gain from the distance to the light cone, we observe that this is not the time resonance case. Intuitively speaking, we have the following type of phase associated with the wave-Vlasov type interaction,

|\xi|-\mu|\eta|-\hat{v}\cdot(\xi-\eta),\quad\mu\in\{+,-\}.

Note that, let
t\in[2^{m-1},2^{m}],

\langle v\rangle\leq 2^{m/10},|\eta|\approx 2^{-m},|\xi|\approx 1,
we have

\big||\xi|-\mu|\eta|-\hat{v}\cdot(\xi-\eta)\big|\gtrsim|\xi|\langle v\rangle^{-2}.

Therefore, we can do form transformation to exploit the non-time-resonance property. As a result, we gain at least
\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}
, which is sufficient.

1.4.2.
Energy estimate for the Vlasov part

For the Vlasov part, we use the framework used by the author in the study of the Vlasov-Nordström system
[
50
]
and the Vlasov-Maxwell system
[
51
]
. We propagate polynomially weighted
L^{2}_{x,v}
-type norm of two sets of vector fields for the Vlasov part.

The first set of vector fields commutes with the Einstein-Vlasov system. The second set of vector fields, which is only applied to the Vlasov part, helps to control the
\nabla_{v}f
, which is the main difficulty of energy estimate for the Vlasov equation because
\nabla_{v}
does not commute with the linear transport operator
\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}
. We should point out that, when the second set of vector fields unavoidably acts on the wave part, it can be reduced to the classical vector fields we propagate for the wave part, see Lemma
2.2
.

Moreover, based on the total number of space derivatives and the total number of the vector fields applied on the profiles of the Vlasov part, we also set the hierarchy for the energy of the Vlasov part.

To be less technical, one key observation is that there exists null structure for the nonlinearity of the Vlasov part. Roughly speaking, we encounter the following type of term in the energy estimate of the Vlasov part,

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{u}_{1}(t,x,v)\omega(x,v)U^{\mu}(t,x+t\hat{v})u_{2}(t,x,v)dxdv,\quad\forall\mu\in\{+,-\},

where
\omega(x,v)
is a weight function,
u_{1}(t,x,v)
is the profile of the Vlasov part with some vector field,
u_{2}(t,x,v)
is the profile of the Vlasov part with some other vector field,
U(t,\cdot)
is the profile of the metric part, and
U^{+}:=U,U^{-}:=\overline{U}.

Let

\mathcal{N}(t,x,v):=u_{1}(t,x,v)\omega(x,v)u_{2}(t,x,v),\quad V:=e^{it|\nabla|}U.

On the Fourier side, we have

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{u}_{1}(t,x,v)\omega(x,v)U^{\mu}(t,x+t\hat{v})u_{2}(t,x,v)dxdv

=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-it\mu|\xi|+it\hat{v}\cdot\xi}\widehat{V^{\mu}}(t,\xi)\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(t,-\xi,v)d\xi dvdt.

Therefore, the time resonance set is given by
\{(\xi,v):|\xi|-\mu\hat{v}\cdot\xi=0\}
.

Roughly speaking, for the acceleration force in the Vlasov equation, see (
1.12
), we have

\hat{v}_{\mu}\hat{v}_{\nu}P_{k}(h_{\mu\nu})=\sum_{U\in\{\textup{profiles of }F,\underline{F},\omega_{l},\vartheta_{mn}\}}\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}\big(T_{k;\mu}^{\tilde{h}}(v)U\big)^{\mu}+\textup{error terms}

(1.16)

where the symbol
T_{k;\mu}^{\tilde{h}}(v)(\xi),\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{l},\vartheta_{mn}\},
of the Fourier multiplier operator
T_{k;\mu}^{\tilde{h}}(v)
vanishes if
|\xi|-\mu\hat{v}\cdot\xi=0
, see (
6.29
) for details.

Therefore, we can do normal form transformation to exploit the benefit of null structure.

1.4.3.
Z
-norm estimates

The Z-norm method, with different choices of the norm itself, depending on the problem, was used in several small data global regularity problems, see
[
13
,
14
,
18
,
29
,
26
,
27
,
28
]
. The
Z
-norm method essentially depends on identifying the “correct” designer
Z
-norm to prove sharp decay estimates for the metric part and the density-type function.

For the good parts, i.e.,
\{F,\omega_{j},\vartheta_{ij}\}
, we show that their
Z
-norms do not grow over time, which implies that the good parts decay sharply at
1/\langle t\rangle
rate over time.
For the bad part
\underline{F}
, it grows at most at rate
\langle t\rangle^{\delta_{1}}
.

We show that the wave-Vlasov type interaction plays minor role in the
Z
-norm analysis. As a result, the modified scattering property of the metric part obtained in
[
29
]
is also valid for the Einstein-Vlasov system.

For the Vlasov part, as suggested from the linear decay estimate of the density type function, see Lemma
3.1
, the
Z
-norm we used for the Vlasov part is same as in
[
50
,
51
]
.

Roughly speaking, we show that the zero-frequency of the profile as well as its
v
-derivatives do not grow over time. As a result, the density function and its derivatives decay sharply.

Because of the zero-output-frequency, which ensures that the size of the input-frequency of the metric part and the size of the input-frequency of the Vlasov part are same, the loss from the normal form transformation can be recovered by the symbol of the Vlasov part. As a result, the decay rate of nonlinearity are not at
1/\langle t\rangle
critical rate. Hence the
Z
-norm of the Vlasov part is uniformly bounded over time.

1.5.
Notation

For any
x\in\cup_{n}\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we use the Japanese bracket
\langle x\rangle
to denote
(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}
. We fix an even smooth function
\tilde{\psi}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
, which is supported in
[-3/2,3/2]
and equals to “
1
” in
[-5/4,5/4]
. For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the cutoff functions
\psi_{k},\psi_{\leq k},\psi_{\geq k}:\cup_{n=1,3}\mathbb{R}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
as follows,

\psi_{k}(x):=\tilde{\psi}(|x|/2^{k})-\tilde{\psi}(|x|/2^{k-1}),\quad\psi_{\leq k}(x):=\tilde{\psi}(|x|/2^{k})=\sum_{l\leq k}\psi_{l}(x),\quad\psi_{\geq k}(x):=1-\psi_{\leq k-1}(x).

Let
P_{k}
,
P_{\leq k}
, and
P_{\geq k}
be the Fourier multiplier operators with symbols
\psi_{k}(\xi),\psi_{\leq k}(\xi),
and
\psi_{\geq k}(\xi)
respectively. We use
f^{+}
to denote
f
and use
f^{-}
to denote
\bar{f}
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we use
f_{k}
to denote
P_{k}f
.

For an integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we use
k_{+}
to denote
\max\{k,0\}
and use
k_{-}
to denote
\min\{k,0\}
. For any unit vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we use
\angle(u,v)
to denote the angle between
u
and
v
and use the convention that
\angle(u,v)\in[0,\pi]
. For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the following set of integers,

\begin{split}\chi_{k}^{1}&:=\{(k_{1},k_{2}):k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z},|k_{1}-k_{2}|\leq 10,k\leq k_{1}+10\},\\
\chi_{k}^{2}&:=\{(k_{1},k_{2}):k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z},k_{1}\leq k_{2}-10,|k-k_{2}|\leq 5\},\\
\chi_{k}^{3}&:=\{(k_{1},k_{2}):k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z},k_{2}\leq k_{1}-10,|k-k_{1}|\leq 5\}.\end{split}

(1.17)

Given a symbol
a=a(x,\xi):\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we define the Weyl quantization operator
T_{a}
as follows,

T_{a}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\mathcal{F}_{x}[a](\xi-\eta,\frac{\xi+\eta}{2})\hat{f}(\eta)\psi_{\leq-100}(\frac{|\xi-\eta|}{|\xi+\eta|})d\eta d\xi.

(1.18)

For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the
\mathcal{S}^{a}
-class of symbols as follows,

\mathcal{S}^{a}:=\{m(\xi):\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-ak+|\alpha|k}\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\xi\rightarrow x}[\nabla_{\xi}^{\alpha}m(\xi)\psi_{k}(\xi)](x)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}<\infty\}.

(1.19)

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we use
\Lambda_{k}[\mathcal{N}]
to denote the
k
-th order term of the nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}
, where the Taylor expansion are in terms of the perturbed metric
h_{\alpha\beta}
and the distribution function
f
of particles. Similarly, we use
\Lambda_{\geq k}[\mathcal{N}]:=\sum_{l\geq k}\Lambda_{l}[\mathcal{N}]
to denote the
k
-th and higher order terms of the nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}
.

1.6.
Organization

The plan of this paper is organized as follows,

\bullet

In section
2
, we introduce the set-up of the problem, classify the nonlinearities of both the metric part and the Vlasov part, and state our main bootstrap assumption.

\bullet

In section
3
, we estimate
L^{2}
-type and
L^{\infty}
-type norms for some basic quantities and reduce the weighted norm estimate to the energy estimates of the wave part and the Vlasov part.

\bullet

In section
4
, we estimate the increment of energy for the perturbed metric components.

\bullet

In section
5
, we estimate the increment of
Z
-norm of the metric component.

\bullet

In section
6
, we estimate the increment of energy for the Vlasov part

\bullet

In section
7
, we estimate the increment of
Z
-norm for the Vlasov part.

2.
Preliminary

2.1.
Vector fields for the Einstein-Vlasov system

For the wave-Vlasov type coupled system, we have the following joint vector fields,

P:=\{S:=t\partial_{t}+x\cdot\nabla_{x},\Omega_{i,j}:=x^{i}\partial_{x^{j}}-x^{j}\partial_{x^{i}},L_{i}:=t\partial_{x^{i}}+x^{i}\partial_{t}\},

(2.1)

\mathcal{P}:=\{t\partial_{t}+x\cdot\nabla_{x},\tilde{\Omega}_{i,j}:=x^{i}\partial_{x^{j}}-x^{j}\partial_{x^{i}}+v_{i}\partial_{v_{j}}-v_{j}\partial_{v_{i}},\tilde{L}_{i}:=t\partial_{x^{i}}+x^{i}\partial_{t}+\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}\partial_{v_{i}}\},

(2.2)

where vector fields in
P
are classic vector fields commute with the wave operator and vector fields in
\mathcal{P}
commute with the linear operator
\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla
of the Vlasov part.

As stated in the following Lemma, by using the above defined vector fields, we observe a favorable structure for the nonlinearity of the Vlasov equation (
1.12
).

Lemma 2.1
.

The following decomposition holds for the following special form,

\nabla_{x}\phi(t,x)\cdot\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)=\big(1+|v|^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\tilde{L}_{i}f-L_{i}\phi\partial_{x^{i}}f+\partial_{t}\phi Sf-S\phi\partial_{t}f\big).

(2.3)

Proof.

Note that

\nabla_{x}\phi(t,x)\cdot\nabla_{v}f(t,x,v)=\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\partial_{v_{i}}f=\big(1+|v|^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\tilde{L}_{i}f-\partial_{x^{i}}\phi(t\partial_{x^{i}}+x^{i}\partial_{t})f\big)

=\big(1+|v|^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\tilde{L}_{i}f-t\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\partial_{x^{i}}f-x^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\partial_{t}f\big)=\big(1+|v|^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\partial_{x^{i}}\phi\tilde{L}_{i}f-L_{i}\phi\partial_{x^{i}}f

+\partial_{t}\phi x^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}f-S\phi\partial_{t}f+t\partial_{t}\phi\partial_{t}f\big)=\big(1+|v|^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\partial_{x_{i}}\phi\tilde{L}_{i}f-L_{i}\phi\partial_{x_{i}}f-S\phi\partial_{t}f+\partial_{t}\phi Sf\big).

Hence finishing the proof of the desired equality (
2.3
).
∎

The key point of the decomposition (
2.3
) is that, as
\nabla_{v}
does not commute with the transport operator
\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x}
, generally speaking, we can not control
\nabla_{v}f
directly. However, thanks to the decomposition (
2.3
), all terms on the right hand side of (
2.3
) depends only on the commutable vector fields.

Definition 2.1
.

For any non-negative integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we define the set of vector fields
P_{n}
as follows,

\begin{split}P_{n}&:=\{\Gamma^{\alpha},\quad\Gamma\in\{\Omega_{ij},S,L_{i},i,j\in\{1,2,3\}\},|\alpha|\leq n\},\\
\mathcal{P}_{n}&:=\{\tilde{\Gamma}^{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\Gamma}\in\{\tilde{\Omega}_{ij},S,\tilde{L}_{i},i,j\in\{1,2,3\}\},|\alpha|\leq n\}.\end{split}

(2.4)

For
L_{1}=\nabla_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}\Gamma_{1}^{\beta_{1}},L_{2}=\nabla_{x}^{\alpha_{2}}\Gamma_{2}^{\beta_{2}}\in P_{n}
, we define
L_{1}\prec L_{2}
(
L_{1}\preceq L_{2}
) if
|\alpha_{1}|\leq|\alpha_{2}|
,
|\beta_{1}|\leq|\beta_{2}|
, and
|\alpha_{1}|+|\beta_{1}|<|\alpha_{2}|+|\beta_{2}|
(
|\alpha_{1}|+|\beta_{1}|\leq|\alpha_{2}|+|\beta_{2}|
). For any
L=\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}\Gamma^{\beta}
, we let
|L|:=|\beta|
and
\|L\|:=|\alpha|+|\beta|
.

We can define
\mathcal{L}_{1}\prec\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{1}\preceq\mathcal{L}_{2}),

\mathcal{L}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{n},i=1,2
, and
|\mathcal{L}|
in a similar fashion. For any
L\in P_{n}
, there exists a uniquely determined vector field
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that for any smooth function
h(t,x)
, we have
Lh=\mathcal{L}h
. We use the convention that
L
and its uniquely determined vector field
\mathcal{L}
are treated same in the
\prec
and
\preceq
relations. For convenience in notation, we define
P_{0}:=\{Id\}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}:=\{Id\}
.

Now, we compute the equations satisfied by the distribution function with vector fields act on it. Recall (
1.12
). We have

(\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x})f=-\Lambda_{\geq 1}[(v^{0})^{-1}]v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\frac{1}{2}(v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\partial_{j}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{j}}f.

(2.5)

Note that, for any
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we have

\mathcal{L}\big(\partial_{j}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{j}}f\big)=\sum_{\mathcal{L}_{1},\mathcal{L}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{a}^{b},\mathcal{L}_{1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{2}\preceq{\mathcal{L}}}c_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{{\mathcal{L}};j,l}(v)\partial_{j}\mathcal{L}_{1}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{l}}\mathcal{L}_{2}f.

Alternatively,
from the equality (
2.3
) in Lemma
2.1
, the following equality holds for some uniquely determined coefficients,

\mathcal{L}\big(\partial_{j}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{j}}f\big)=\mathcal{L}\big(\big(1+|v|^{2}\big)^{-1/2}\big(\partial_{x^{i}}g^{\alpha\beta}\tilde{L}_{i}f-L_{i}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{x^{i}}f+\partial_{t}g^{\alpha\beta}Sf-Sg^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{t}f\big)

=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{L}_{1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{2}\preceq{\mathcal{L}},(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in P\times\{\partial_{\alpha}\}\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\}\times\mathcal{P}\end{subarray}}c_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{L};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)\Gamma_{1}\mathcal{L}_{1}f\Gamma_{2}\mathcal{L}_{2}g^{\alpha\beta},\quad|c_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{L};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)|\lesssim(1+|v|)^{-1}.

(2.6)

Moreover, to highlight the role of extra derivatives in quasilinear terms, we point out that the following equalities hold,

c_{id\mathcal{L}}^{{\mathcal{L}};j,l}(v)\partial_{j}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{l}}\mathcal{L}f=\sum_{\Gamma_{1}\in P\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\},\Gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{P}\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\}}c_{id{\mathcal{L}}}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)\Gamma_{1}\mathcal{L}f\Gamma_{2}g^{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{x}g^{\alpha\beta}\cdot\nabla_{v}{\mathcal{L}}f,

(2.7)

c_{\mathcal{L}id}^{{\mathcal{L}};j,l}(v)\partial_{j}\mathcal{L}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{v_{l}}f=\sum_{\Gamma_{1}\in P\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\},\Gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{P}\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\}}c_{{\mathcal{L}}id}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)\Gamma_{1}f\Gamma_{2}\mathcal{L}g^{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{x}{\mathcal{L}}g^{\alpha\beta}\cdot\nabla_{v}f.

(2.8)

Therefore, from the above equalities (
2.5
–
2.8
), we can write the Vlasov equation schematically as follows,

(\partial_{t}+\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{v})\mathcal{L}f=\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\nabla_{v}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\mathcal{N}_{4}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v),

(2.9)

where

\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\mathcal{L}}=-\Lambda_{\geq 1}[(v^{0})^{-1}]v\mathcal{L}f,\quad\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=-\frac{1}{2}(v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\nabla_{x}g^{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{L}f,

(2.10)

\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L}}=-\frac{1}{2}(v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{L}g^{\alpha\beta}\cdot\nabla_{v}f=\nabla_{v}\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L};1}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L};2}},\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L};1}}:=-\frac{1}{2}(v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{L}g^{\alpha\beta}f

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L};2}}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{v}\big((v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\big)\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathcal{L}g^{\alpha\beta}f,

\mathcal{N}_{4}^{\mathcal{L}}=\sum_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{2}\preceq{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{L}_{2}\prec\mathcal{L}}\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{L}}(v)\Lambda_{\geq 1}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(v^{0})^{-1}]\cdot\nabla_{x}\mathcal{L}_{2}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\Lambda_{\geq 1}[(v^{0})^{-1}](t,x)\mathcal{L}f(t,x,v)

+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{L}_{1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{2}\circ\mathcal{L}_{3}\preceq{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{L}_{2}\prec{\mathcal{L}},(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in P\times\{\partial_{\alpha}\}\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\}\times\mathcal{P}\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)\Gamma_{1}\mathcal{L}_{1}g^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{2}\mathcal{L}_{2}f\mathcal{L}_{3}\big(({2v^{0}})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\big),

(2.11)

where the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{L}}(v)
and
\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)
satisfy the following estimate,

(1+|v|)|\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}}(v)|+|\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{L}}(v)|\lesssim 1.

(2.12)

For any vector
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we define

u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v):={\mathcal{L}}f(t,x+\hat{v}t,v)
to be the profile of the Vlasov part, which is the pull-back of the nonlinear solution along the linear flow.

Recall (
2.9
). As a result of direct computations, we rewrite the nonlinearity of
\partial_{t}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
in terms of profiles as follows,

\partial_{t}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+D_{v}\cdot\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\mathfrak{N}_{3}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\mathfrak{N}_{4}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v),

(2.13)

where

D_{v}:=\nabla_{v}-t\nabla_{v}\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x},\quad\mathfrak{N}_{i}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=\mathcal{N}_{i}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x+t\hat{v},v),\quad i\in\{1,\cdots,4\}.

(2.14)

To control the energy of
\nabla_{v}^{\alpha}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
, which provides the control of the
L^{\infty}_{x}
-norm of the density type functions,
we also utilize a second set of vector fields for the Vlasov equation, see
[
50
]
for more details about the the construction of the second set of vector fields. More precisely, we define

K_{v}:=\nabla_{v}-\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}\omega(x,v)\nabla_{v}\hat{v}\cdot\nabla_{x},

(2.15)

S^{v}:=\tilde{v}\cdot\nabla_{v},\quad S^{x}:=\tilde{v}\cdot\nabla_{x},\quad\Omega^{v}_{i}=\tilde{V}_{i}\cdot\nabla_{v},\quad\Omega^{x}_{i}=\tilde{V}_{i}\cdot\nabla_{x},

(2.16)

\widehat{S}^{v}:=\tilde{v}\cdot K_{v}=S^{v}-\frac{\omega(x,v)}{{1+|v|^{2}}}S^{x},\quad\widehat{\Omega}^{v}_{i}:=\tilde{V}_{i}\cdot K_{v}=\Omega_{i}^{v}-\omega(x,v)\Omega_{i}^{x},\quad K_{v_{i}}:=K_{v}\cdot e_{i},

(2.17)

where

\omega(x,v)=\psi_{\geq 0}(|x|^{2}+(x\cdot v)^{2})\big(x\cdot v+\sqrt{(x\cdot{v})^{2}+|x|^{2}}\big),

(2.18)

\tilde{V}_{i}=e_{i}\times\tilde{v},\quad\tilde{v}:=\frac{v}{|v|},\quad\tilde{v}_{i}:=\tilde{v}\cdot e_{i},\quad\hat{v}_{i}:=\hat{v}\cdot e_{i},\quad e_{1}:=(1,0,0),e_{2}:=(0,1,0),e_{3}:=(0,0,1).

(2.19)

With the above defined vector fields, the following favorable decomposition holds,

D_{v}=\sum_{\iota\in\mathcal{S},|\iota|=1}c_{\iota}(t,x,v)\Lambda^{\iota},\quad|c_{\iota}(t,x,v)|\lesssim(1+|v|)||t|-|x+t\hat{v}||.

(2.20)

We use the following notation to represent the above vector fields uniformly,

\begin{split}&\Gamma_{1}=\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\widehat{S}^{v},\quad\Gamma_{2}:=\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)S^{x},\quad\Gamma_{i+2}:=\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\widehat{\Omega}_{i}^{v},\\
&\Gamma_{i+5}:=\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\Omega^{x}_{i},\quad\Gamma_{i+8}:=\psi_{\leq 0}(|v|)K_{v_{i}},\quad\Gamma_{i+11}:=\psi_{\leq 0}(|v|)\partial_{x_{i}},\quad i=1,2,3,\\
&\mathcal{K}:=\{\vec{e}:\,\,\vec{e}\in\{0,1\}^{14},|\vec{e}|=0,1\},\quad\vec{0}:=(0,\cdots,0),\vec{e}_{i}:=(0,\cdots,\overbrace{1}^{\text{$i$-th}},\cdots,0),\\
&\textit{if\,\,}\vec{0},\vec{e}_{i}\in\mathcal{K},\quad\Lambda^{\vec{{0}}}:=Id,\quad\Lambda^{\vec{e}_{i}}:=\Gamma_{i},\quad\mathcal{S}:=\cup_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\mathcal{K}^{k},\quad\forall e,f\in\mathcal{S},\Lambda^{e\circ f}:=\Lambda^{e}\Lambda^{f}.\end{split}

(2.21)

For any
\beta\in\mathcal{S}
, we have
\beta\thicksim\iota_{1}\circ\cdots\iota_{|\beta|},\iota_{i}\in\mathcal{K}/\{\vec{0}\}
. We let
|\beta|
to denote total number of derivatives. Moreover, we define the index “
c(\beta)
” to distinguishes the velocity derivative in radial direction from the the velocity derivative in rotational directions and define the index “
\tilde{c}(\beta)
” to count the total number of derivatives in
v
in
\Lambda^{\beta}
as follow,

\begin{split}&c(\beta)=\sum_{i=1,\cdots,|\beta|}c(\iota_{i}),\quad c(\iota)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\textup{if\,}\Lambda^{\iota}\thicksim\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\widehat{S}^{v}\\
-1&\textup{if\,}\Lambda^{\iota}\thicksim\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\widehat{\Omega}^{v}_{i},i\in\{1,2,3\}\\
0&\textup{otherwise}\\
\end{array}\right.,\iota\in\mathcal{K}/\{\vec{0}\},\\
&\tilde{c}(\beta):=|\{\iota_{i}:\,\,\beta\sim\iota_{1}\circ\iota_{2}\circ\cdots\iota_{|\beta|},\iota_{i}\sim\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\widehat{S}^{v},\textup{or},\,\psi_{\geq 1}(|v|)\widehat{\Omega}_{i}^{v},\textup{or},\,\psi_{\leq 0}(|v|)K_{v_{j}},j\in\{1,2,3\}\}|.\end{split}

(2.22)

To compute the equation satisfied by
\Lambda^{\alpha}u^{\mathcal{L}}
, by using the following Lemma, we know that, if the second set of vector fields act on the perturbed metric component, they can be represented by the classic vector fields in
P
, see (
2.1
).

Lemma 2.2
.

The following identity holds for any
\rho\in\mathcal{S},

\Lambda^{\rho}\big(f(t,x+\hat{v}t)\big)=\sum_{L\in P_{|\rho|}}{c}_{\rho}^{L}(t,x,v)Lf(t,x+\hat{v}t),

(2.23)

where the coefficients
{c}_{\rho}^{L}(x,v)
,
L\in P_{|\rho|}
, satisfy the following estimate,

|t\partial_{t}{c}_{\rho}^{L}(t,x,v)|+|{c}_{\rho}^{L}(t,x,v)|\lesssim(1+|x|)^{|\rho|-|L|}(1+|v|)^{|\rho|-|L|-c(\rho)}.

(2.24)

Moreover, the following rough estimate holds for any
\kappa\in\mathcal{S},

|\Lambda^{\kappa}\big({c}_{\rho}^{L}(t,x,v)\big)|\lesssim(1+|x|)^{|\kappa|+|\rho|-|L|}(1+|v|)^{|\kappa|+|\rho|-|L|-c(\rho)}.

(2.25)

Proof.

See
[
50
]
[Lemma 4.1]
∎

From the equation satisfied by
u^{\mathcal{L}}
in (
2.13
) and the equality (
2.23
) in Lemma
2.2
, we classify the equation satisfied by
\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}
as follows,

\partial_{t}\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=\nabla_{x}\cdot\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,1}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+D_{v}\cdot\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,2}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,3}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,4}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v),

(2.26)

where
\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,1}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
and
\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,2}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
reveal the symmetric structure of the equation
\partial_{t}\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
, in which all vector fields act on the profile of the Vlasov part,
\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,3}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
denotes terms in which all vector fields act on the metric part, lastly,
\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,4}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)
denotes the rest terms.

More precisely, we have

\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,1}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=-\Lambda_{\geq 1}[(v^{0})^{-1}](t,x+t\hat{v})v\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v),

(2.27)

\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,2}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=-\frac{1}{2}\big((v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\nabla_{x}g^{\alpha\beta}\big)(t,x+t\hat{v})\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v),

(2.28)

\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,3}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{L}\in\cup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}P_{l}\\
|\tilde{L}|\leq\tilde{c}(\rho),|\alpha|\leq|\rho|-\tilde{c}(\rho)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2}c^{\tilde{L}}_{\rho}(x,v)\big((v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\nabla_{x}\tilde{L}\mathcal{L}g^{\alpha\beta}\big)(t,x+t\hat{v})\cdot D_{v}u(t,x,v),

(2.29)

\mathfrak{N}_{\rho,4}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{1}\circ\rho_{2}\preceq\rho,\tilde{L}\in\cup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}P_{l},\mathcal{L}_{1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{2}\preceq{\mathcal{L}}\\
\mathcal{L}_{1}\prec\mathcal{L},|\tilde{L}|\leq\tilde{c}(\rho_{2}),|\alpha|\leq|\rho_{2}|-\tilde{c}(\rho_{2})\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2};\tilde{L}}^{\mathcal{L};\rho,\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}(x,v)\cdot\nabla_{x}\Lambda^{\rho_{1}}u^{\mathcal{L}_{1}}(t,x,v)

\times\Lambda_{\geq 1}[\tilde{L}\mathcal{L}_{2}(v^{0})^{-1}](t,x+t\hat{v},v)

+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{L}_{1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{2}\circ\mathcal{L}_{3}\preceq{\mathcal{L}},\rho_{1}\circ\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{3}\preceq\rho,\tilde{L}_{i}\in\cup_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3},l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\nabla_{x}^{\alpha_{i}}P_{l},\\
|\tilde{L}_{i}|\leq\tilde{c}(\rho_{i+1}),|\alpha_{i}|\leq|\rho_{i+1}|-\tilde{c}(\rho_{i+1}),i\in\{1,2\},\\
|\tilde{c}(\rho_{i})|+|\mathcal{L}_{i}|<|\rho|+|\mathcal{L}|,(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in P\times\{\partial_{\alpha}\}\cup\{\partial_{\alpha}\}\times\mathcal{P}\\
\end{subarray}}c_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{3};\tilde{L}_{1},\tilde{L}_{2}}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\rho,\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}(x,v)\tilde{L}_{1}\Gamma_{2}\mathcal{L}_{2}g^{\alpha\beta}(t,x+t\hat{v})

\times\Lambda^{\rho_{1}}u^{\Gamma_{1}\mathcal{L}_{1}}(t,x,v)\tilde{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{3}\big(({2v^{0}})^{-1}{v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}}\big)(t,x+t\hat{v},v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\Lambda_{\geq 1}[(v^{0})^{-1}](t,x+t\hat{v})

\times\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)+\frac{1}{2}D_{v}\cdot\big(\big((v^{0})^{-1}v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}\nabla_{x}g^{\alpha\beta}\big)(t,x+t\hat{v})\big)\Lambda^{\rho}u^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v),

(2.30)

where, from the estimates (
2.24
) and (
2.25
) in Lemma
2.2
, the coefficients satisfy the following point-wise estimates,

\begin{split}|c^{\tilde{L}}_{\rho}(x,v)|&\lesssim(1+|x|)^{|\rho|-|\tilde{L}|}(1+|v|)^{|\rho|-|\tilde{L}|-c(\rho)},\\
|\tilde{c}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2};\tilde{L}}^{\mathcal{L};\rho,\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}(x,v)|&\lesssim(1+|x|)^{|\rho_{2}|-|\tilde{L}|}(1+|v|)^{1+|\rho_{2}|-|\tilde{L}|-c(\rho_{2})},\\
|c_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}\mathcal{L}_{3};\tilde{L}_{1},\tilde{L}_{2}}^{{\mathcal{L}};\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\rho,\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}}(x,v)|&\lesssim(1+|x|)^{|\rho_{2}|+|\rho_{3}|-|\tilde{L}_{1}|-|\tilde{L}_{2}|}(1+|v|)^{|\rho_{2}|+|\rho_{3}|-|\tilde{L}_{1}|-|\tilde{L}_{2}|-c(\rho_{2})-c(\rho_{3})}.\end{split}

(2.31)

2.2.
Double Hodge decomposition of the metric tensor

Firstly, we rewrite the Einstein equations in terms of the perturbation with respect to the Minkowski spacetime. Define

h_{\alpha\beta}=g_{\alpha\beta}-\eta_{\alpha\beta},\qquad\Longrightarrow g^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}-\eta^{\alpha\gamma}h_{\gamma\lambda}\eta^{\lambda\beta}+\,\,\textup{quadratic and higher order terms}.

(2.32)

Hence, from (
1.6
), we have

\square h_{\alpha\beta}=H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}h_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}+\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl},\quad\square:=\partial_{t}^{2}-\Delta,

(2.33)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}
denotes the semilinear nonlinearity of wave-wave type interaction and
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}
denotes the semilinear nonlinearity of wave-Vlasov type interaction. More precisely, the detailed formulas of
H_{i\gamma}
,
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}
are given as follows,

H_{i\gamma}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}2(g^{00})^{-1}\Lambda_{\geq 1}[g^{\gamma i}]&\textup{if}\,\,\gamma=0\\
&\\
\Lambda_{\geq 1}[(g^{00})^{-1}]\delta_{i}^{\gamma}+(g^{00})^{-1}\Lambda_{\geq 1}[g^{i\gamma}]&\textup{if}\,\,\gamma\in\{1,2,3\},\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.34)

\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}=-\big(g^{00}\big)^{-1}(P_{\alpha\beta}+Q_{\alpha\beta}),\quad\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}=\big(g^{00}\big)^{-1}(T_{\alpha\beta}(f)-\frac{1}{2}g_{\alpha\beta}T(f)).

(2.35)

For any
{L}\in\cup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{3},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}P_{n}
, recall (
2.33
), we have

\square Lh_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{\tilde{L}\in\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}P_{c}^{d},\tilde{L}\preceq L}c_{L\tilde{L}}\tilde{L}\big(H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}h_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}+\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}\big).

(2.36)

To exploit the harmonic gauge condition, following the study of the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system in
[
29
]
, for any
L\in P_{n},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we define

F^{L}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(Lh_{00}+R_{i}R_{j}Lh_{ij}\big),\quad\underline{F}^{L}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(Lh_{00}-R_{i}R_{j}Lh_{ij}\big),\quad\rho:=R_{i}Lh_{0i},\\
\omega_{j}:=\varepsilon_{jkl}R_{k}{L}h_{0l},\quad\Omega_{j}^{{L}}:=\varepsilon_{jkl}R_{k}R_{m}{L}h_{lm},\quad\vartheta_{jk}:=\varepsilon_{jmp}\varepsilon_{knq}R_{m}R_{n}Lh_{pq}.

(2.37)

We can recover the perturbation of metric
Lh_{\alpha\beta}
from the above defined double Hodge decomposition,

Lh_{00}=F^{L}+\underline{F}^{L},\quad Lh_{0j}=-R_{j}\rho^{L}+\varepsilon_{jkl}R_{k}\omega_{l}^{L}\\
Lh_{jk}=R_{j}R_{k}\big(F^{L}-\underline{F}^{L}\big)-\big(\varepsilon_{klm}R_{j}+\varepsilon_{jlm}R_{k}\big)R_{l}\Omega_{m}^{L}+\varepsilon_{jpm}\varepsilon_{kqn}R_{p}R_{q}\vartheta_{mn}^{L}.

(2.38)

To represent the connection between the perturbed metric and the double Hodge decomposition variables schematically, we know that there exist uniquely determined zero order operators
R_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}},R^{\alpha\beta}_{\tilde{h}}\in\{R_{i},R_{i}R_{j}\}\times\mathbb{C}
such that the following equalities holds,

Lh_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\Omega_{j},\rho,\vartheta_{mn}\}}R_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}}\tilde{h}^{L},\quad\tilde{h}^{L}=R^{\alpha\beta}_{\tilde{h}}L_{\alpha\beta},\quad\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\Omega_{j},\rho,\vartheta_{mn}\}.

(2.39)

Due to the harmonic coordinate gauge condition, the variables
\rho
,
\Omega_{j}
can be recovered from the other defined variables. Hence, it would be sufficient to control
\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}
over time to control the perturbed metric. More precisely, we have

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
R_{0}:=\partial_{t}|\nabla|^{-1}
. For any
{L}\in P_{n},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we define

E^{comm}_{{L},\mu}:=\eta^{\alpha\beta}[\partial_{\alpha},{L}]h_{\beta\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\alpha\beta}[\partial_{\mu},{L}]h_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{\tilde{L}\prec L}c_{L;\tilde{L}}^{\mu,\nu;\alpha,\beta}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{L}h_{\alpha\beta},\\
E_{\mu}:=\eta^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\beta\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}=-\Lambda_{\geq 2}[\Gamma_{\mu}].

(2.40)

Then the following equalities hold for the variables
\rho^{{L}}
and
\omega_{j}^{{L}}
,

\rho^{{L}}=R_{0}\big(\underline{F}^{{L}}+\tau^{{L}}\big)+|\nabla|^{-1}\big({L}E_{0}+E^{comm}_{{L},0}\big),\quad\tau^{{L}}=-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{jk}\vartheta^{{L}}_{jk},\\
\Omega_{j}^{{L}}:=R_{0}\omega_{j}^{{L}}+\varepsilon_{jlk}|\nabla|^{-1}R_{l}\big({L}E_{k}+E_{{L},k}^{comm}\big),

(2.41)

where
\tau^{{L}}
satisfies the following equalities

2|\nabla|^{2}\tau^{{L}}=\partial_{\alpha}{L}E_{\alpha}+\partial_{\alpha}E^{comm}_{{L},\alpha}-\frac{1}{2}\big(\square{L}h_{00}+R_{i}R_{j}\square{L}h_{ij}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{jk}\varepsilon_{jmp}\varepsilon_{knq}R_{m}R_{n}\square{L}h_{pq},

(2.42)

2|\nabla|\partial_{t}\tau^{{L}}=-|\nabla|{L}E_{0}+R_{k}\partial_{t}{L}E_{k}-|\nabla|E_{{L},0}^{com}+\partial_{t}R_{k}E_{{L},k}^{comm}-R_{i}\square{L}h_{0i}.

(2.43)

Proof.

See
[
29
]
[Lemma 6.7].
∎

Remark 2.1
.

Recall (
2.42
) and (
2.43
). Unlike the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system studied in
[
29
]
, a key difference in the Einstein-Vlasov system is that
\tau^{\mathcal{L}}
and
\partial_{t}\tau^{\mathcal{L}}
in the Einstein-Vlasov system contain linear density type term which depends on the Vlasov part. More precisely,

\Lambda_{1}[\tau^{\mathcal{L}}]=\frac{1}{4}|\nabla|^{-2}\Lambda_{1}[\big(-\big(\square\mathcal{L}h_{00}+R_{i}R_{j}\square\mathcal{L}h_{ij}\big)+\delta_{jk}\varepsilon_{jmp}\varepsilon_{knq}R_{m}R_{n}\square\mathcal{L}h_{pq}\big)]

=\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}|\nabla|^{-2}M_{\alpha\beta}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\mathcal{L}f(t,x,v)dv\big),

(2.44)

\Lambda_{1}[\partial_{t}\tau^{\mathcal{L}}]=-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla|^{-1}R_{i}\Lambda_{1}[\square\mathcal{L}h_{0i}]=\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}|\nabla|^{-1}R_{i}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{0i}(v)\mathcal{L}f(t,x,v)dv\big),

(2.45)

where
M_{\alpha\beta}\in\{c_{1}R_{i},c_{2}R_{i}R_{j},i,j\in\{1,2,3,\},c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}\}.

Recall the double Hodge decomposition in (
2.37
). From the equation (
2.36
), we classify schematically the nonlinearities of
\square\tilde{h}^{L}
as follows,

\square\tilde{h}^{L}=R_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}^{L}}(\square Lh_{\alpha\beta})=\sum_{\tilde{L}\in\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}P_{c}^{d},\tilde{L}\preceq L}c_{L\tilde{L}}R_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}^{L}}\big(\tilde{L}\big(H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}h_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}+\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}\big)\big)

=H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{h}^{L}+N_{vl}^{{\tilde{h};L}}+R_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L}+Q_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L},\quad\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\},

(2.46)

where the uniquely determined linear operator
R_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}^{L}}\in\{R_{i},R_{i}R_{j},i,j\in\{1,2,3\}\}\times\mathbb{C}
,
N_{vl}^{{\tilde{h};L}}
denotes the nonlinearity that depends on the Vlasov part,
Q_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L}
denotes the quadratic terms of the wave-wave type, and
R_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L}
denotes the cubic and higher order nonlinearity which only depends on the metric component.

2.3.
Modified profile for the perturbed metric

Since the non-linearity
N_{vl}^{{\tilde{h};L}}
in (
2.46
) contains linear level terms which depend only on the Vlasov part, to capture the linear effect, in this subsection, we define corresponding modified profile of the perturbed metric.

Roughly speaking, the
modified
profile of the perturbed metric has similar property of the original (un-modified) profile of the perturbed metric in the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system
[
29
]
as it takes out the leading effect of the Vlasov part on the perturbed metric.

Firstly, for
L\in P_{n}
,
\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\rho,\Omega_{j},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}
, we define the profiles of
\tilde{h}^{L}
and
Lh_{\alpha\beta}
as follows,

V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}=e^{it|\nabla|}U^{\tilde{h}^{L}},\quad U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}:=(\partial_{t}-i|\nabla|)\tilde{h}^{L},\quad\Longrightarrow\partial_{t}V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}=e^{it|\nabla|}\big(\square\tilde{h}^{L}\big),

(2.47)

V^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}=e^{it|\nabla|}U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}},\quad U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}:=(\partial_{t}-i|\nabla|)Lh_{\alpha\beta},\quad\Longrightarrow\partial_{t}V^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}=e^{it|\nabla|}\big(\square Lh_{\alpha\beta}\big),

(2.48)

\tilde{h}^{L}=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}c_{\mu}|\nabla|^{-1}\big(U^{L\tilde{h}}(t)\big)^{\mu},\quad\partial_{t}\tilde{h}^{L}=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}\big(U^{L\tilde{h}}(t)\big)^{\mu},\quad c_{\mu}:=i\mu/2.

(2.49)

Next, we identify the linear density type functions in
N_{vl}^{{\tilde{h};L}}
. Recall (
2.46
).
As a typical example, we compute the linear term of
N_{vl}^{F;L}
as follows,

\begin{split}\Lambda_{1}[N_{vl}^{F;L}]=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{L}\in\cup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}P_{n},\hat{L}\preceq L\end{subarray}}c_{L\hat{L}}\big[\hat{L}\big(\Lambda_{1}[-T_{00}(f)+\frac{1}{2}g_{00}T(f)\big)]\\
&+R_{i}R_{j}\big(\hat{L}\big(\Lambda_{1}[-T_{ij}(f)+\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}T(f)\big)]\big)\big].\end{split}

(2.50)

Note that, for any
L\in P_{n}
and the corresponding vector field
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, the following equality of general form holds for some determined coefficients,

L-\mathcal{L}=\sum_{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}\prec\mathcal{L},|\alpha|+|\tilde{\mathcal{L}}|\leq|\mathcal{L}|}a_{\alpha\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{L}(v)\nabla_{v}^{\alpha}\tilde{\mathcal{L}},\quad|a_{\alpha\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{L}(v)|\lesssim\langle|v|\rangle^{|\alpha|}.

(2.51)

After using the above equality (
2.51
) and doing integration by parts in “
v
”, the following equality holds for any fixed
L\in P_{n}
,

L\big(-\Lambda_{1}[T_{\alpha\beta}(f)]\big)=L\big(-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(t,x,v)\frac{\Lambda_{0}[v_{\alpha}]\Lambda_{0}[v_{\beta}]}{\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}}dvdv\big)\\
=\sum_{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq\mathcal{L},|\mu|+|\tilde{\mathcal{L}}|\leq|\mathcal{L}|}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}a_{\mu\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}^{L}(v)\nabla_{v}^{\mu}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}f(x^{\alpha},v)\frac{\Lambda_{0}[v_{\alpha}]\Lambda_{0}[v_{\beta}]}{\sqrt{1+|v|^{2}}}dv=\sum_{\tilde{\mathcal{L}}\preceq\mathcal{L}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\tilde{a}^{L\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\tilde{\mathcal{L}}f(t,x,v)dv,

(2.52)

where “
\tilde{a}^{L\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)
” are some determined coefficients that satisfy the following estimate,

\sum_{|\alpha|\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\|\langle|v|\rangle^{|\alpha|-10}\nabla_{v}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}^{L\tilde{\mathcal{L}}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\lesssim 1.

(2.53)

The reduction we did in (
2.52
) is standard and it will be used directly later when we apply the macroscopic vector fields to the density type variables associated with the Vlasov part. With minor modifications, we know that the following equality holds for some uniquely determined coefficients
{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)
,

\Lambda_{1}[L\mathcal{N}_{vl}^{h_{\alpha\beta}}]=\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\mathcal{L}f(t,x,v)dv,\quad\sum_{|\alpha|\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\|\langle|v|\rangle^{|\alpha|-10}\nabla_{v}^{\alpha}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\lesssim 1.

(2.54)

Similar to the process we did for the linear term in (
2.52
), recall (
2.35
) and (
1.9
), we can formulate the quadratic term of
L\big(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}\big)
as follows for some uniquely determined coefficients
{c}^{L{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}_{\alpha\beta;\gamma\kappa}(v)
,

\Lambda_{2}[L\big(\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{vl}\big)]=\sum_{|{L}_{1}|+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|\leq|L|}L_{1}{h}_{\gamma\kappa}\big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{c}^{L{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}_{\alpha\beta;\gamma\kappa}(v)\mathcal{L}_{2}f(t,x,v)dv\big),

(2.55)

where the following estimate holds for the coefficients
{c}^{L{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}_{\alpha\beta;\gamma\kappa}(v)
,

\sum_{|\alpha|\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\|\langle|v|\rangle^{|\alpha|-10}\nabla_{v}^{\alpha}{c}^{L{L}_{1}\mathcal{L}_{2}}_{\alpha\beta;\gamma\kappa}(v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}}\lesssim 1.

(2.56)

Lastly, we are ready to define the modified profiles of the perturbed metric. To exploit the fact that the speed of particle is strictly less than the speed of light and balance out the linear effect caused by the Vlasov part in the equations satisfied by the perturbed metric,
\forall\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\rho,\Omega_{j},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}
we define

\begin{split}\widehat{\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}}(t,\xi)&:=\widehat{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t,\xi)-\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{it|\xi|-it\hat{v}\cdot\xi}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{h}}(v)\big(i(|\xi|-\hat{v}\cdot\xi)\big)^{-1}{k_{\tilde{h}}(\xi)\widehat{u^{\mathcal{L}}}(t,\xi,v)}dv,\\
\widehat{\widetilde{V^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}}(t,\xi)&:=\widehat{V^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t,\xi)-\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{it|\xi|-it\hat{v}\cdot\xi}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\big(i(|\xi|-\hat{v}\cdot\xi)\big)^{-1}{\widehat{u^{\mathcal{L}}}(t,\xi,v)}dv.\end{split}

(2.57)

Recall the equation satisfied by
\tilde{h}^{L}
in (
2.46
). From the equalities (
2.47
) and (
2.57
), we highlight the quasilinear terms, which are responsible for losing derivatives, as follows,

\begin{split}e^{-it|\nabla|}\partial_{t}\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}&=H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}{\partial_{\gamma}\tilde{h}^{L}}+\Lambda_{\geq 2}[N_{vl}^{{\tilde{h};L}}]+R_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L}+Q_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L}+\mathcal{N}_{vl;m}^{{\tilde{h};L}},\\
\mathcal{N}_{vl;m}^{{\tilde{h};L}}&=\sum_{\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}_{a}^{b},|\mathcal{L}|\leq|L|}-\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-it\hat{v}\cdot\xi}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\frac{k^{\tilde{h}}_{\alpha\beta}(\xi)\partial_{t}\widehat{u^{\mathcal{L}}}(t,\xi,v)}{i2\big(|\xi|-\hat{v}\cdot\xi\big)}dv\big].\end{split}

(2.58)

Correspondingly, we define a modified half wave variables and the modified perturbed metric as follows,

\widetilde{U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t,x):=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}[e^{-it|\xi|}\widehat{\widetilde{V^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}}(t,\xi)](x),

(2.59)

\forall\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\rho,\Omega_{j},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{ij}\},\quad\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t,x):=\mathcal{F}_{\xi}^{-1}[e^{-it|\xi|}\widehat{\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}}(t,\xi)](x),

(2.60)

\widetilde{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}(t):=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}c_{\mu}|\nabla|^{-1}\big(\widetilde{U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t)\big)^{\mu},\quad\widetilde{\tilde{h}^{L}}(t):=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}c_{\mu}|\nabla|^{-1}\big(\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big)^{\mu},

(2.61)

\widetilde{\partial_{t}Lh_{\alpha\beta}}(t):=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}\frac{\big(\widetilde{U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t)\big)^{\mu}}{2}\qquad\widetilde{\partial_{t}\tilde{h}^{L}}(t):=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}\frac{\big(\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big)^{\mu}}{2},

(2.62)

\tilde{\rho}^{h_{\alpha\beta}}_{L}(f)(t):=U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}(t)-\widetilde{U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t),\quad\tilde{\rho}^{\tilde{h}}_{L}(f)(t):=U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)-\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t),

(2.63)

\widetilde{\rho}^{h_{\alpha\beta}}_{L}(f)(t):=\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-it\hat{v}\cdot\xi}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha\beta}(v)\big(i|\xi|-i\hat{v}\cdot\xi\big)^{-1}\big(\widehat{u^{\mathcal{L}}}(t,\xi,v)\big)dv\big](t,x),

(2.64)

\widetilde{\rho}^{\tilde{h}}_{L}(f)(t):=\sum_{\mathcal{L}\preceq L}\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-it\hat{v}\cdot\xi}{b}^{L\mathcal{L}}_{\tilde{h}}(v)k_{\tilde{h}}(\xi)\big(i|\xi|-i\hat{v}\cdot\xi\big)^{-1}\big(\widehat{u^{\mathcal{L}}}(t,\xi,v)\big)dv\big](t,x).

(2.65)

We remark that, from the above definition, the modified perturbed metric components are all real valued functions. In particular, although
\widetilde{\partial_{t}\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)
and
\partial_{t}\widetilde{\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)
are different, they are same at the linear level. From (
2.64
), as a result of direct computation, we have

\partial_{t}\widetilde{\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)-\widetilde{\partial_{t}\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}c_{\mu}|\nabla|^{-1}\big((\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big)^{\mu}=\textup{Im}\big[|\nabla|^{-1}e^{-it|\nabla|}\partial_{t}\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big],

(2.66)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\widetilde{\partial_{t}\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)&=\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}\frac{1}{2}\big(-i|\nabla|\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big)^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\big(e^{-it|\nabla|}\partial_{t}\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big)^{\mu}\\
&=\Delta\widetilde{\tilde{h}^{L}}(t)+\sum_{\mu\in\{+,-\}}\frac{1}{2}\big(e^{-it|\nabla|}\partial_{t}\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\big)^{\mu}.\end{split}

(2.67)

From the equality in (
2.39
) and results in Lemma
2.3
, we can reformulate the profile
{U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t)
in terms of the modified profile of the essential parts
\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t,x)
,
\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}
as follows,

{U^{Lh_{\alpha\beta}}}(t)=\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}}\tilde{R}_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}}\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)+\sum_{\tilde{L}\prec L}R_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma\kappa}\widetilde{U^{\tilde{L}h_{\gamma\kappa}}}+Err_{\alpha\beta}^{L;wa}(t)+Err_{\alpha\beta}^{L;vl}(t),

(2.68)

where the low order error type terms comes from the commutators
E_{{L},\alpha}^{comm}
in Lemma
2.3
. More precisely,

\tilde{R}_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{R}_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}}&\textup{if\,\,}\tilde{h}\in\{F,\vartheta_{mn}\}\\
{R}_{\alpha\beta}^{\underline{F}}R_{\gamma}+iR_{\alpha\beta}^{\rho}&\textup{if\,\,}\tilde{h}=\underline{F}\\
{R}_{\alpha\beta}^{\omega_{j}}R_{\gamma}+iR_{\alpha\beta}^{\Omega_{j}}&\textup{if\,\,}\tilde{h}=\omega_{j},\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.69)

Err_{\alpha\beta}^{L;wa}(t)=R_{\alpha\beta}^{\rho}\big(|\nabla|^{-1}\Lambda_{\geq 2}[\square\underline{F}^{L}]+|\nabla|^{-1}(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)({L}E_{0})+|\nabla|^{-1}\big(\Lambda_{\geq 2}[\square\tau^{L}]+i\Lambda_{\geq 2}[(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)\tau^{L}]\big)\big)

+R_{\alpha\beta}^{\Omega_{j}}\big(|\nabla|^{-1}\Lambda_{\geq 2}[\square\omega_{j}^{L}]+|\nabla|^{-1}(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)\varepsilon_{jlk}R_{l}({L}E_{k})\big),

(2.70)

Err_{\alpha\beta}^{L;vl}(t)=\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\rho,\Omega_{j},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}}\sum_{\tilde{L}\preceq L}R_{\alpha\beta}^{\tilde{h}}\tilde{\rho}^{\tilde{h}}_{\tilde{L}}(f)(t)+R_{\alpha\beta}^{\rho}\big(|\nabla|^{-1}(\Lambda_{1}[\square(\underline{F}^{L}+\underline{\tau}^{L})]

+i\Lambda_{1}[(\partial_{t}+i|\nabla|)\tau^{L}]\big)+|\nabla|^{-1}R_{\alpha\beta}^{\Omega_{j}}\Lambda_{1}[\square\omega_{j}^{L}].

(2.71)

2.4.
Classification of quadratic terms of the perturbed metric

For convenience in later argument, in this subsection, we elaborate and classify the quadratic terms of the equations satisfied by perturbed metric component in (
2.46
). Depending on the roughness allowed or the delicacy required to close the bootstrap argument, in different scenarios, we use different strategies to decompose the quadratic terms.
Recall (
2.33
), (
2.35
), the detailed formulas of
P_{\alpha\beta}
and
Q_{\alpha\beta}
in (
1.7
) and (
1.8
). For any
{L}\in\cup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{3},n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}P_{n},
we have

{L}(\Lambda_{2}[\mathcal{N}_{\alpha\beta}^{wa}])+L(\Lambda_{2}[H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}h_{\alpha\beta}]):=-L\big(\Lambda_{2}[P_{\alpha\beta}]+\Lambda_{2}[Q_{\alpha\beta}]\big)+L\Lambda_{2}[S_{\alpha\beta}],\\
L(\Lambda_{2}[K_{\alpha\beta}])=\sum_{L_{1}\circ L_{2}\preceq L,k\in\mathbb{Z},(k_{1},k_{2})\in\chi_{k}^{1}\cup\chi_{k}^{2}\cup\chi_{k}^{3}}c_{L_{1}L_{2}}^{L}QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}},\quad K\in\{S,P,Q\}.

(2.72)

In particular, we single out the term that is responsible for losing derivatives, which is the case all vector fields hit on the quasilinear part, i.e.,
L_{2}=L
,

c_{IdL}^{L}QS_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;Id,L}=\Lambda_{1}[H_{i\gamma}]\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}Lh_{\alpha\beta}.

(2.73)

The detailed formulas of
QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}},K\in\{S,Q,P\}
, are given as follow,

QS_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{k}\big[P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{00})P_{k_{2}}(\Delta L_{2}h_{\alpha\beta})-2P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{0i})P_{k_{2}}(\partial_{t}\partial_{i}L_{2}h_{\alpha\beta})&\\
+P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{ij})P_{k_{2}}(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}L_{2}h_{\alpha\beta})\big]&\textup{if}\,\,|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|=|L|\\
&\\
c^{\mu\nu}_{i\gamma}P_{k}\big(P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{\mu\nu})P_{k_{2}}(\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}L_{2}h_{\alpha\beta})\big)&\textup{if}\,\,|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|\leq|L|,\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.74)

QQ_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}c^{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}_{\gamma,\kappa;\iota,\tau,1}P_{k}\big(Q_{\mu,\nu}(P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{\gamma\kappa}),P_{k_{2}}(L_{2}h_{\iota\tau}))\big)&\textup{if}\,\,|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|=|L|\\
&\\
c^{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}_{\gamma,\kappa;\iota,\tau,2}P_{k}\big(\partial_{\mu}P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{\gamma\kappa})\partial_{\nu}P_{k_{2}}(L_{2}h_{\iota\tau})\big)&\textup{if}\,\,|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|\leq|L|,\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.75)

QP_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\gamma\mu}\eta^{\lambda\nu}P_{k}\big[2\big(-\partial_{\alpha}P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{\mu\lambda})P_{k_{2}}(\partial_{\beta}L_{2}h_{\gamma\nu})\big)&\\
+\partial_{\alpha}P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{\mu\gamma})\partial_{\beta}P_{k_{2}}(L_{2}h_{\lambda\nu})\big]&\textup{if}\,\,|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|=|L|\\
&\\
c^{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}_{\gamma,\kappa;\iota,\tau,3}P_{k}\big(\partial_{\mu}P_{k_{1}}(L_{1}h_{\gamma\kappa})\partial_{\nu}P_{k_{2}}(L_{2}h_{\iota\tau})\big)&\textup{if}\,\,|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|\leq|L|,\\
\end{array}\right.

(2.76)

and the null bilinear operators
Q_{\alpha,\beta}(\cdot,\cdot),\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1,2,3\},
are defined as follows,

Q_{0,0}(f,g):=\partial_{t}f\partial_{t}g-\nabla_{x}f\cdot\nabla_{x}g,\quad Q_{\alpha,i}(f,g)=\partial_{\alpha}f\partial_{i}g-\partial_{i}f\partial_{\alpha}g.

(2.77)

Recall the relation in (
2.39
) and the equation satisfied by
\tilde{h}^{L}
in (
2.46
). We have

Q_{mt}^{\tilde{h};L}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z},(k_{1},k_{2})\in\chi_{k}^{1}\cup\chi_{k}^{2}\cup\chi_{k}^{3}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\circ L_{2}\preceq L,\tilde{L}_{1}\circ\tilde{L}_{2}\preceq L,L_{2}\prec L,H\in\{P,Q\}\\
\end{subarray}}R^{\alpha\beta}_{\tilde{h}}QS_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}+R^{\alpha\beta}_{\tilde{h}}QH_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;\tilde{L}_{1},\tilde{L}_{2}}.

(2.78)

In our bootstrap argument, we allow the Vlasov part to grow faster than the modified metric part. Due to different growth rates, to better measure the growth rate of quadratic terms, we split the perturbed metric into the modified perturbed metric and the density type function. As a result, generally speaking, we have the following structural decomposition,

QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}=QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{ess;L,L_{1},L_{2}}+QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{vl;L,L_{1},L_{2}}+QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{err;L,L_{1},L_{2}},

(2.79)

where
QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{ess;L,L_{1},L_{2}}
denotes the essential part of the wave-wave type interaction, in which the inputs are modified perturbed metric instead of the original perturbed metric,
QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{vl;L,L_{1},L_{2}}
denotes the wave-Vlasov type interaction, and
QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{err;L,L_{1},L_{2}}
denotes the error type term.

The detailed formulas of the above decomposition change depend on the size of
|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|
and the roughness allowed. For the rough cases, we use the modified perturbed metric variables in (
2.64
). As a result, for any
K\in\{S,P,Q\}
, we have

QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{ess;L,L_{1},L_{2}}=\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\{+,-\}}P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu;ess}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}((\widetilde{U_{k_{1}}^{L_{1}h_{\iota\tau}}})^{\mu},(\widetilde{U_{k_{2}}^{L_{2}h_{\gamma\kappa}}})^{\nu}),\quad QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{err;L,L_{1},L_{2}}=0,

(2.80)

QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{vl;L,L_{1},L_{2}}=\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\{+,-\}}P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu;vl}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}((\widetilde{U_{k_{1}}^{L_{1}h_{\iota\tau}}})^{\mu},P_{k_{2}}(\tilde{\rho}_{L_{2}}^{h_{\gamma\kappa}})^{\nu})

+P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu;vl}^{L;L_{1},L_{2}}(P_{k_{1}}(\tilde{\rho}_{L_{1}}^{h_{\iota\tau}}))^{\mu},({U_{k_{2}}^{L_{2}h_{\gamma\kappa}}})^{\nu}).

(2.81)

Alternatively, for some delicate cases, we use the modified double Hodge decomposition variables. Recall the detailed formulas in (
2.74
), (
2.75
), and (
2.76
). We substitute the perturbed metric by its corresponding half wave defined in (
2.48
) and using the equality (
2.68
), the following decomposition holds for any
K\in\{P,Q,S\},

QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{ess;L,L_{1},L_{2}}=\sum_{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}}P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}((\widetilde{U^{\tilde{h}_{1}^{L_{1}}}_{k_{1}}}(t))^{\mu},(\widetilde{U_{k_{2}}^{\tilde{h}_{2}^{L_{2}}}}(t))^{\nu})),

(2.82)

QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{err;L,L_{1},L_{2}}:=\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}}\sum_{\mu,\nu\in\{+,-\}}P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}\big((({U_{k_{1}}^{\tilde{h}^{L_{1}}}}(t))^{\mu},P_{k_{2}}(Err_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}^{L_{2};wa}(t))^{\nu})\big)

+\sum_{\tilde{L}_{i}\preceq L_{i},|\tilde{L}_{1}|+|\tilde{L}_{2}|<|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|}P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{L}_{1},\tilde{L}_{2};\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1},\gamma_{2}\kappa_{2}}((\widetilde{U^{\tilde{L}_{1}h_{\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1}}}_{k_{1}}}(t))^{\mu},(\widetilde{U_{k_{2}}^{\tilde{L}_{2}h_{\gamma_{2}\kappa_{2}}}}(t))^{\nu}))

+P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}\big(P_{k_{1}}(Err_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}^{L_{1};wa}(t))^{\mu}),(\widetilde{U_{k_{2}}^{\tilde{h}^{L_{2}}}}(t))^{\nu}\big),

(2.83)

QK_{\alpha\beta;k,k_{1},k_{2}}^{vl;L,L_{1},L_{2}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}\\
\mu,\nu\in\{+,-\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\tilde{L}_{i}\preceq L_{i},|\tilde{L}_{1}|+|\tilde{L}_{2}|<|L_{1}|+|L_{2}|}P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}\big((({U_{k_{1}}^{\tilde{h}^{L_{1}}}}(t))^{\mu},P_{k_{2}}(Err_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}^{L_{2};vl}(t))^{\nu})\big)

+P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}\big(P_{k_{1}}(Err_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}^{L_{1};vl}(t))^{\nu}),(\widetilde{U_{k_{2}}^{\tilde{h}^{L_{2}}}}(t))^{\nu}\big)

+P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{L}_{1},\tilde{L}_{2};\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1},\gamma_{2}\kappa_{2}}((P_{k_{1}}(\widetilde{\rho}^{h_{\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1}}}_{\tilde{L}_{1}}(f)(t)))^{\mu},(\widetilde{U_{k_{2}}^{\tilde{L}_{2}h_{\gamma_{2}\kappa_{2}}}}(t))^{\nu}))

+P_{k}QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{L}_{1},\tilde{L}_{2};\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1},\gamma_{2}\kappa_{2}}(({U^{\tilde{L}_{1}h_{\gamma_{1}\kappa_{1}}}_{k_{1}}}(t))^{\mu},(P_{k_{2}}(\widetilde{\rho}^{h_{\gamma_{2}\kappa_{2}}}_{\tilde{L}_{2}}(f)(t)))^{\nu})).

(2.84)

2.5.
Null structure of the Einstein’s equations

The null structure inside the Einstein equations, in particular, its form on the Fourier side has been studied in details by Ionescu-Pausader in
[
29
]
. We record main conclusions here and refer readers to
[
29
]
for more details. Let

\mathcal{M}:=\{m:(\mathbb{R}^{3}/\{0\})^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}:\quad m(x,y)=m_{1}(x,y)m^{\prime}(x+y),\quad m^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\\
|x|^{\alpha}|\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}m^{\prime}(x)|\lesssim_{\alpha}1,|x|^{\alpha}|y|^{\beta}|\nabla_{x}^{\alpha}\nabla_{y}^{\beta}m_{1}(x,y)|\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}1,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}\}.

(2.85)

Moreover, we define the following classes of null multipliers,

\begin{split}\mathcal{M}_{\pm}^{null}&:=\{n:(\mathbb{R}^{3}/\{0\})^{2}:n(x,y):=(\frac{x_{i}}{|x|}\mp\frac{y_{i}}{|y|})m(x,y),m\in\mathcal{M},\textup{for some \,}i\in\{1,2,3\}\},\\
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}^{null}&:=\{n:(\mathbb{R}^{3}/\{0\})^{2}:n(x,y):=(\frac{x_{i}+y_{i}}{|x+y|}\mp\frac{y_{i}}{|y|})m(x,y),m\in\mathcal{M},\textup{for some \,}i\in\{1,2,3\}\}.\end{split}

(2.86)

For
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R},k,k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{k,k_{1},k_{2}}&:=\{m:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\eta}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\|m(\xi-\eta,\eta)\|_{\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{k,k_{1},k_{2}}}:=\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}}2^{|\alpha|k_{1}+|\beta|k_{2}}\\
&\times\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\xi,\eta}[\nabla_{\xi}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\eta}^{\beta}m(\xi-\eta,\eta)\psi_{k}(\xi)\psi_{k_{1}}(\xi-\eta)\psi_{k_{2}}(\eta)](x,y)\|_{L^{1}_{x,y}}<+\infty\}\\
P_{v}^{d}\mathcal{M}^{a;b,c}&:=\{m:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\xi}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{\eta}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\|m(\xi-\eta,\eta,v)\|_{P_{v}^{d}\mathcal{M}^{a;b,c}}:=\sup_{k,k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}}\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3}}2^{-ak-bk_{1}-ck_{2}}\\
&\times\|\langle v\rangle^{-d}\mathcal{F}^{-1}_{\xi,\eta}[\nabla_{\xi}^{\alpha}\nabla_{\eta}^{\beta}m(\xi-\eta,\eta,v)\psi_{k}(\xi)\psi_{k_{1}}(\xi-\eta)\psi_{k_{2}}(\eta)](x,y)\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{x,y}}<\infty\}.\end{split}

(2.87)

As summarized in the following Lemma
2.4
, all quadratic terms have null structure except the
Q(\vartheta_{mn},\vartheta_{pq})
type quadratic term in the equation satisfied by the component
\underline{F}.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
qk_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}(\xi-\eta,\eta),k\in\{s,p,q\}
, be the symbol of the bilinear operators
QK_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}
in
(
2.83
)
. For any
\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\},\mu,\nu\in\{+,-\}
, we have
qs_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\in|\xi-\eta||\eta|^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}\cup|\xi-\eta|^{-1}|\eta|\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null},
and
qq_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}.
Moreover, if
\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}\notin\{\vartheta_{mn}\}
, we have
qp_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}.
If
\tilde{h}\neq\underline{F}
and
\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}\in\{\vartheta_{mn}\}
, we have
R_{\tilde{h}}^{\alpha\beta}(\xi)qp_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{-\nu}^{null}
where
R_{\tilde{h}}^{\alpha\beta}(\xi)
is the symbol of the Fourier multiplier operator
R_{\tilde{h}}^{\alpha\beta}
defined in
(
2.39
)
.

Proof.

The above property depends solely on the Einstein equation, which has been verified and used in the study of the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system. We only provide an intuitive explanation here,
see
[
29
]
for more details.

As a result of direct computations,
\Lambda_{2}[H_{i\gamma}\partial_{i}\partial_{\gamma}h_{\alpha\beta}]
,
\Lambda_{2}[P_{\alpha\beta}]

+1/2\partial_{\alpha}R_{p}R_{q}\vartheta_{mn}\partial_{\beta}R_{p}R_{q}\vartheta_{mn}
, and
\Lambda_{2}[Q_{\alpha\beta}]
, are linear combinations of null forms with symbols in
\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}\cup|\xi-\eta||\eta|^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}\cup\xi-\eta|^{-1}|\eta|\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}
. For quasilinear term, the corresponding symbol belongs to
|\xi-\eta||\eta|^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}\cup\xi-\eta|^{-1}|\eta|\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}
. For semilinear term, the corresponding symbol belongs to
\mathcal{M}_{\mu\nu}^{null}
.

Moreover, let
f=R_{p}R_{q}\vartheta_{mn}
, we note that
\partial_{0}f\partial_{0}f+R_{i}R_{j}(\partial_{i}f\partial_{j}f)
,
\varepsilon_{jkl}R_{k}\big(\partial_{0}f\partial_{l}f\big)
, and
\varepsilon_{jmp}\varepsilon_{knq}

R_{m}R_{n}(\partial_{p}f\partial_{q}f)
are all null forms with symbol in
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{-\nu}^{null}
, i.e.,
R_{\tilde{h}}^{\alpha\beta}(\xi)qp_{\alpha\beta;\mu\nu}^{\tilde{h}_{1},\tilde{h}_{2}}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{-\nu}^{null}
, where
\tilde{h}\in\{F,\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}
.

That is to say, all quadratic terms have null structure except the
Q(\vartheta_{mn},\vartheta_{pq})
type quadratic term in the equation satisfied by the component
\underline{F}.

∎

2.6.
Bootstrap assumption

Let
\delta_{1}:=10^{-20}
,
\gamma:=\delta_{1}/100
,
{\delta}_{0}:=100\delta_{1},
and
N_{0}=200
. Our bootstrap assumption for the perturbed metric component is stated as follows,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{n,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},n+l\leq N_{0}}\sum_{{L}\in{P}_{n}}\langle t\rangle^{-d(n,l)\delta_{0}}\|U^{{L}h_{\alpha\beta}}(t))\|_{E_{l}}+\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\{F,\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}}\langle t\rangle^{-e(|L|;\tilde{h})\delta_{1}}\|\widetilde{V^{\tilde{h}^{L}}}(t)\|_{E_{l}}

+\langle t\rangle^{-e(|L|;\underline{F})\delta_{1}}\|\widetilde{V^{\underline{F}^{L}}}(t)\|_{E_{l}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{L}\in{P}_{l},l\leq N_{0}-10\\
i\in\{1,2,3\}\\
\end{subarray}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle t\rangle^{-{H}(l+1)\delta_{1}}2^{k-k_{-}/2+\gamma k_{-}+(N_{0}-l-1)k_{+}}

\times\|P_{k}(x_{i}V^{\tilde{L}h_{\alpha\beta}})(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|{V^{\tilde{h}}}(t)\|_{Z}+\langle t\rangle^{-\delta_{1}}\|{V^{\underline{F}}}(t)\|_{Z}\lesssim\epsilon_{1}:=\epsilon_{0}^{5/6},

(2.88)

where the energy space
E_{l}
is defined in (
1.15
), the
Z
-normed space is defined as follows,

\|f\|_{Z}:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{1.001k_{-}+40k_{+}}\|\widehat{f}(\xi)\psi_{k}(\xi)\|_{L^{\infty}_{\xi}},

and
d(n,l)
,
e(|L|;\tilde{h}),\tilde{h}\in\{F,\underline{F},\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}
, and
H(n),n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are defined as follows

e(|L|;\tilde{h}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H(|L|)&\tilde{h}=\underline{F}\\
H(|L|)-\beta(|L|)&\tilde{h}\in\{F,\omega_{j},\vartheta_{mn}\}\\
\end{array}\right.,\quad d(n,l):=H(n+l)+10n,

H(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}30n+3&n<2\\
200n^{2}&n\geq 2\\
\end{array}\right.,\quad\beta(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1+1/50&\textup{if\,}n<2\\
H(1)+1/50&\textup{if\,}n\geq 2\\
\end{array}\right..

(2.89)

The choice of function
H(n)
guarantees the following estimates hold,

\begin{split}\textup{if\,\,}a\leq b,&\quad H(a+1)+H(b)\leq H(a+b)+H(1),\\
\textup{if\,\,}n\geq 1,&\quad H(n)-H(n-1)\geq 20\beta(n).\end{split}

(2.90)

The bootstrap assumption we stated in (
2.88
) and the energy spaces we use are stronger than the one used in
[
29
]
, i.e., the bootstrap assumption stated in (
2.88
) is automatically satisfied. The
Z
-normed space is same as the one used in
[
29
]
. This setting allows us to take the analysis of the increment of
Z
-norm of the perturbed metric contributed from the Einstein part as a black box and be more focus on the overall effect of the Vlasov part, which is the main theme of this paper.

Recall the equation (
2.26
) and the index
\tilde{c}(\beta),\beta\in\mathcal{S},
defined in (
2.22
). Our bootstrap assumption for the Vlasov part is stated as follows,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{L}\in\cup_{l\leq N_{0}}\mathcal{P}_{l},\rho\in\mathcal{S},|\mathcal{L}|+|\rho|\leq N_{0}\end{subarray}}(1+t)^{-H(\mathcal{L},\rho)\delta_{0}}E_{\mathcal{L},\rho}^{vl}(t)+Z^{vl}(t)\lesssim\epsilon_{1},

(2.91)

where
E_{\mathcal{L},\rho}^{vl}(t)
aims to control the wighted energy of the Vlasov part and the
Z
-norm of the Vlasov part
Z^{vl}(t)
aims to show that the density of particles and its derivatives decay sharply over time in a low regularity space. More precisely,

H(\mathcal{L},\rho):=d(|\mathcal{L}|+|\tilde{c}(\rho)|,|\rho|-|\tilde{c}(\rho)|),\qquad E_{\mathcal{L},\rho}^{vl}(t):=\|\omega^{\mathcal{L}}_{\rho}(t,x,v)\Lambda^{\rho}{u}^{\mathcal{L}}(t,x,v)\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}},\\
Z^{vl}(t):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{L}\in\cup_{l\leq N_{0}}\mathcal{P}_{l},\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{3},|\alpha|+|\mathcal{L}|\leq N_{0}\end{subarray}}\|\widetilde{\omega^{\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha}}(v)\big(\nabla_{v}^{\alpha}\widehat{u^{\mathcal{L}}}(t,0,v)-\nabla_{v}\cdot{u^{\mathcal{L};\alpha}_{corr}}(t,v)\|_{L^{2}_{x,v}},

(2.92)

where the above weight functions
\omega^{\mathcal{L}}_{\rho}(t,x,v)
, and
\widetilde{\omega^{\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha}}(v)
are defined as follows,
